But lately I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like… I don’t know, like everything’s kind of flat inside.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can still smile and ask people how their breakfast was or help them get settled, and then I’m thinking about whether I need to pick up detergent on the way home, or if I left my lunch container in the break room again… sorry, I’m rambling. But yeah, underneath all that, I’ve been feeling pretty sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. Not in a big, obvious way, I guess. I mean, everybody worries, right? Like bills, work stuff, whether I remembered to put gas in the car before my shift—actually I almost ran out last week and had to stop at that station with the broken card reader, which was annoying.\n\nBut, uh… no, I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been unusually anxious. I haven’t really noticed that as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stress in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. My uncle died about two weeks ago, and that’s kind of when this whole shift started. I mean, we weren’t together every single day or anything, but he was around a lot when I was younger, and then there’s all the family phone calls and people asking about arrangements and who’s bringing what… it just kind of throws everything off, you know?\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it started after that.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has it been making it hard for you to do your usual work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still showing up to work, so it’s not like I’ve just stopped functioning or anything. But I’ve been having to ask coworkers to double-check my client notes and documentation, because I keep worrying I missed some little detail.\n\nAnd at home, my wife’s been picking up more of the bills and scheduling stuff—appointments, due dates, that kind of thing. Usually I can handle that, or at least my share of it, but lately I’m just not as reliable with the details. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings and concentration problems seem mainly connected to your uncle’s death and everything around it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, that’s when I noticed everything kind of shift—after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. Before that I was… I don’t know, more like myself, I guess.\n\nAnd it’s not even just the funeral stuff, it’s all the calls and little decisions, and my cousin keeps texting in this group chat at like midnight—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, the timing lines up with his death.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this reaction has felt stronger than you would expect for yourself after your uncle’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that, honestly. Like, I didn’t expect to feel great about it or anything—he was family—but I also keep thinking, am I making too much of it? Or is it just all the extra stuff around it?\n\nMy wife said something about how everyone processes things differently, and then my aunt keeps calling with these little questions about food for people coming over, and I get pulled into that and then I’m like… wait, what was I even upset about originally?\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I expected, but I’m not sure I have a good measuring stick for it.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or go run an errand, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether I forgot to return a call, or I’ll start sorting mail and end up reading some old coupon flyer for ten minutes.\n\nSo… enjoying less? Maybe? I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more like my head’s not staying with the thing long enough for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most mornings lately I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I can still get up and go to my shift and talk to people, do the normal stuff, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be helping someone or answering a question and part of me is doing the routine, and then another part is just… not really there. Yeah, sleep’s been kind of… all over the place lately. Some nights I’m just lying there thinking about family stuff, and then it’s like one thought turns into another, and suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said last month or whether I forgot to do something at work.

And other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before the alarm and then I just can’t really settle back down. I’ll look at the clock, which I know you’re not supposed to do, and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell back asleep right then… which of course doesn’t help. So, yeah, I’ve been having some trouble with it.

But lately I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like… I don’t know, like everything’s kind of flat inside.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can still smile and ask people how their breakfast was or help them get settled, and then I’m thinking about whether I need to pick up detergent on the way home, or if I left my lunch container in the break room again… sorry, I’m rambling. But yeah, underneath all that, I’ve been feeling pretty sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. Not in a big, obvious way, I guess. I mean, everybody worries, right? Like bills, work stuff, whether I remembered to put gas in the car before my shift—actually I almost ran out last week and had to stop at that station with the broken card reader, which was annoying.\n\nBut, uh… no, I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been unusually anxious. I haven’t really noticed that as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stress in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. My uncle died about two weeks ago, and that’s kind of when this whole shift started. I mean, we weren’t together every single day or anything, but he was around a lot when I was younger, and then there’s all the family phone calls and people asking about arrangements and who’s bringing what… it just kind of throws everything off, you know?\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it started after that.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has it been making it hard for you to do your usual work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still showing up to work, so it’s not like I’ve just stopped functioning or anything. But I’ve been having to ask coworkers to double-check my client notes and documentation, because I keep worrying I missed some little detail.\n\nAnd at home, my wife’s been picking up more of the bills and scheduling stuff—appointments, due dates, that kind of thing. Usually I can handle that, or at least my share of it, but lately I’m just not as reliable with the details. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings and concentration problems seem mainly connected to your uncle’s death and everything around it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, that’s when I noticed everything kind of shift—after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. Before that I was… I don’t know, more like myself, I guess.\n\nAnd it’s not even just the funeral stuff, it’s all the calls and little decisions, and my cousin keeps texting in this group chat at like midnight—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, the timing lines up with his death.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this reaction has felt stronger than you would expect for yourself after your uncle’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that, honestly. Like, I didn’t expect to feel great about it or anything—he was family—but I also keep thinking, am I making too much of it? Or is it just all the extra stuff around it?\n\nMy wife said something about how everyone processes things differently, and then my aunt keeps calling with these little questions about food for people coming over, and I get pulled into that and then I’m like… wait, what was I even upset about originally?\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I expected, but I’m not sure I have a good measuring stick for it.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or go run an errand, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether I forgot to return a call, or I’ll start sorting mail and end up reading some old coupon flyer for ten minutes.\n\nSo… enjoying less? Maybe? I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more like my head’s not staying with the thing long enough for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most mornings lately I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I can still get up and go to my shift and talk to people, do the normal stuff, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be helping someone or answering a question and part of me is doing the routine, and then another part is just… not really there. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it lost, exactly. Like, I still do some of the usual things, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them the same way or if I’m just kind of doing them because that’s what I normally do.\n\nI tried watching one of those shows I usually like, and then I ended up thinking about whether I needed to buy paper towels, which reminded me of this time at work where we ran out of gloves and everyone made it a whole thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, I guess… maybe a little less pleasure, but I can’t say for sure. It’s been hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Um… appetite? I don’t think I’ve really noticed a big change. I mean, I still eat, but sometimes I don’t pay much attention to it until later, like I’ll realize I had coffee and then got busy with something at home or thinking about work stuff.\n\nBut decrease… I don’t know. Not in a way that jumped out at me. My groceries look about the same, I guess, though I did buy those frozen burritos and then forgot they were in there—sorry, that’s not really the point. So… no, I’m not sure there’s been a decrease.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of… all over the place lately. Some nights I’m just lying there thinking about family stuff, and then it’s like one thought turns into another, and suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said last month or whether I forgot to do something at work.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before the alarm and then I just can’t really settle back down. I’ll look at the clock, which I know you’re not supposed to do, and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell back asleep right then… which of course doesn’t help. So, yeah, I’ve been having some trouble with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sorry, that’s probably not a very straight answer.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling worthless lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / personal_care_aide / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep replaying what I should have done for my tío before he died, like calling more or visiting sooner, and I end up feeling like I failed him as family.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Not like I think I’m worthless all the time, but I keep going back over stuff with my tío before he died. Like, why didn’t I call more, why didn’t I go see him sooner, that kind of thing.

And then my mind starts making a whole list, you know? Like I’ll be washing a cup or something and suddenly I’m back thinking about whether I failed him as family. I know people say you can’t change the past, and I’ve said that to other people before, but it doesn’t really stop it from landing heavy. So… yeah, I do feel pretty guilty about that.

Like, I still do some of the usual things, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them the same way or if I’m just kind of doing them because that’s what I normally do.\n\nI tried watching one of those shows I usually like, and then I ended up thinking about whether I needed to buy paper towels, which reminded me of this time at work where we ran out of gloves and everyone made it a whole thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, I guess… maybe a little less pleasure, but I can’t say for sure. It’s been hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Um… appetite? I don’t think I’ve really noticed a big change. I mean, I still eat, but sometimes I don’t pay much attention to it until later, like I’ll realize I had coffee and then got busy with something at home or thinking about work stuff.\n\nBut decrease… I don’t know. Some nights I’m just lying there thinking about family stuff, and then it’s like one thought turns into another, and suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said last month or whether I forgot to do something at work.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before the alarm and then I just can’t really settle back down. I’ll look at the clock, which I know you’re not supposed to do, and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell back asleep right then… which of course doesn’t help. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings and concentration problems seem mainly connected to your uncle’s death and everything around it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, that’s when I noticed everything kind of shift—after my uncle died, about two weeks ago. Before that I was… I don’t know, more like myself, I guess.\n\nAnd it’s not even just the funeral stuff, it’s all the calls and little decisions, and my cousin keeps texting in this group chat at like midnight—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, the timing lines up with his death.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this reaction has felt stronger than you would expect for yourself after your uncle’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that, honestly. Like, I didn’t expect to feel great about it or anything—he was family—but I also keep thinking, am I making too much of it? Or is it just all the extra stuff around it?\n\nMy wife said something about how everyone processes things differently, and then my aunt keeps calling with these little questions about food for people coming over, and I get pulled into that and then I’m like… wait, what was I even upset about originally?\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I expected, but I’m not sure I have a good measuring stick for it.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or go run an errand, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether I forgot to return a call, or I’ll start sorting mail and end up reading some old coupon flyer for ten minutes.\n\nSo… enjoying less? Maybe? I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more like my head’s not staying with the thing long enough for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most mornings lately I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I can still get up and go to my shift and talk to people, do the normal stuff, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be helping someone or answering a question and part of me is doing the routine, and then another part is just… not really there. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it lost, exactly. Like, I still do some of the usual things, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them the same way or if I’m just kind of doing them because that’s what I normally do.\n\nI tried watching one of those shows I usually like, and then I ended up thinking about whether I needed to buy paper towels, which reminded me of this time at work where we ran out of gloves and everyone made it a whole thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, I guess… maybe a little less pleasure, but I can’t say for sure. It’s been hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Um… appetite? I don’t think I’ve really noticed a big change. I mean, I still eat, but sometimes I don’t pay much attention to it until later, like I’ll realize I had coffee and then got busy with something at home or thinking about work stuff.\n\nBut decrease… I don’t know. Not in a way that jumped out at me. My groceries look about the same, I guess, though I did buy those frozen burritos and then forgot they were in there—sorry, that’s not really the point. So… no, I’m not sure there’s been a decrease.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of… all over the place lately. Some nights I’m just lying there thinking about family stuff, and then it’s like one thought turns into another, and suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said last month or whether I forgot to do something at work.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before the alarm and then I just can’t really settle back down. I’ll look at the clock, which I know you’re not supposed to do, and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell back asleep right then… which of course doesn’t help. So, yeah, I’ve been having some trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not planning to hurt myself or anything like that. I don’t have some plan.\n\nBut… yeah, there have been a few mornings where I wake up and it’s like, this really heavy thought of, “Would it be so bad if I just hadn’t woken up?” And then I kind of scare myself because I don’t usually think like that.\n\nIt’s not that I want to go do something, it’s more like… not wanting to have to keep dealing with everything for a minute, I guess. Sorry, that’s probably not a very straight answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Not like I think I’m worthless all the time, but I keep going back over stuff with my tío before he died. Like, why didn’t I call more, why didn’t I go see him sooner, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd then my mind starts making a whole list, you know? Like I’ll be washing a cup or something and suddenly I’m back thinking about whether I failed him as family. I know people say you can’t change the past, and I’ve said that to other people before, but it doesn’t really stop it from landing heavy. So… yeah, I do feel pretty guilty about that.\n\nDoctor: Before your uncle died, were you already feeling depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really before, no… I mean, I had normal stress, work stuff, family stuff, you know how it piles up, but this low, guilty, foggy feeling — that’s been more like the last month or so.\n\nIt’s hard because time gets weird when something like that happens. I’ll think, “Was I already like this?” and then I remember I was still going to work, still doing my routine, stopping for coffee at the same place… anyway, no, I’d say it’s been hanging over me about a month now.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, like people saying I’m talking slower or anything.\n\nI mean, some days I feel like I take longer to get going, but then I’ll still end up doing normal stuff, and sometimes I’m just distracted. Like yesterday I stood in the kitchen trying to remember why I opened the cabinet, but that happens to everybody, right? My cousin does that all the time and she’s younger than me.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m moving or talking noticeably slower, at least nobody’s pointed it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m running marathons anyway, but even on days where I barely do much, my body feels kind of worn out.\n\nLike at work, helping someone shower — which I’ve done a million times — lately it takes more out of me than it should. Or carrying groceries in, I’ll get inside and feel like, “Why am I so tired from that?” My neighbor, she carries everything in one trip, and I always joke about that, but now I’m doing two trips and still feeling drained. So yeah, low energy, I guess.\n\nDoctor: For at least two full weeks, have these depressive feelings been present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it really kind of shifted after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago.\n\nBefore that, I had off days, sure — everybody does — but this particular feeling, like this cloud over everything, that’s been since then. It’s funny, I keep marking time by the funeral stuff and who brought food over, like my aunt made this casserole nobody could identify… sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nSo, yeah, about two weeks. Pretty much since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Lately, have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that, like people saying I’m talking slower or anything.\n\nI mean, some days I feel like I take longer to get going, but then I’ll still end up doing normal stuff, and sometimes I’m just distracted. Like yesterday I stood in the kitchen trying to remember why I opened the cabinet, but that happens to everybody, right? My cousin does that all the time and she’s younger than me.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m moving or talking noticeably slower, at least nobody’s pointed it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m running marathons anyway, but even on days where I barely do much, my body feels kind of worn out.\n\nLike at work, helping someone shower — which I’ve done a million times — lately it takes more out of me than it should. Or carrying groceries in, I’ll get inside and feel like, “Why am I so tired from that?” My neighbor, she carries everything in one trip, and I always joke about that, but now I’m doing two trips and still feeling drained. So yeah, low energy, I guess.\n\nDoctor: For at least two full weeks, have these depressive feelings been present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it really kind of shifted after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago.\n\nBefore that, I had off days, sure — everybody does — but this particular feeling, like this cloud over everything, that’s been since then. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to answer that. I miss him, yeah, but “longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something out of an old movie.

I’ve thought about him a lot, especially because people keep bringing him up, and my aunt keeps calling about little things from the house — she asked me about a set of tools he had, and then that turned into a whole thing about who borrowed what years ago. My family does that, we can’t answer one simple question without making it a committee meeting.

But wishing I could be with him… I’m not sure I’d put it like that. I miss him. That’s probably the clearest way I can say it.

Maybe a little stronger than I expected, but I’m not sure I have a good measuring stick for it.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I still do things, like I’ll put something on TV or go run an errand, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether I forgot to return a call, or I’ll start sorting mail and end up reading some old coupon flyer for ten minutes.\n\nSo… enjoying less? It’s more like my head’s not staying with the thing long enough for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like I can still get up and go to my shift and talk to people, do the normal stuff, but inside I feel kind of sad and flat.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be helping someone or answering a question and part of me is doing the routine, and then another part is just… not really there. So, yeah, I’d say it’s been there a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it lost, exactly. Like, I still do some of the usual things, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them the same way or if I’m just kind of doing them because that’s what I normally do.\n\nI tried watching one of those shows I usually like, and then I ended up thinking about whether I needed to buy paper towels, which reminded me of this time at work where we ran out of gloves and everyone made it a whole thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, I guess… maybe a little less pleasure, but I can’t say for sure. It’s been hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Um… appetite? I don’t think I’ve really noticed a big change. Some nights I’m just lying there thinking about family stuff, and then it’s like one thought turns into another, and suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said last month or whether I forgot to do something at work.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before the alarm and then I just can’t really settle back down.
